Um… anxious? I don’t know. I mean, I get worried about normal stuff, I guess—like deadlines or whether I’ve messed something up. But I’m not sure I’d say it’s like… all the time.

So I guess I’m looking at it, but I’m not always fully… there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Um… anxious? I don’t know. So… maybe some worry, but I haven’t really noticed it as a main thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself deeply focused on a single task or activity for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. Yeah, I think it happens outside of studying too, though the easiest example is still when I’m at my desk. Like I’ll be trying to read or write something and then a siren goes by outside, or I hear people talking in the hallway, and I’m just… off it.

So it feels like I’m busy with one general area, but I’m not always staying on the exact task I started with. Does that count, or do you mean like losing track of time on one specific thing?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted like this in other areas of your life, not just when you're working or studying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it happens outside of studying too, though the easiest example is still when I’m at my desk. I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as especially restless, like I’m not pacing around all the time or anything.

Sitting still is… it depends. If I’m in a lecture or waiting somewhere, I might shift around or mess with a pen, but I don’t always notice it until someone points it out. Actually, pens are kind of dangerous for me because I’ll start clicking one and then remember this one professor who banned them in class because everyone was clicking during exams—sorry, that’s not really the point.

So, um… maybe a little fidgety sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big thing.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do, especially when it’s something I already thought I checked. That’s actually kind of what finally made me come in.

I keep turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes that are really embarrassing afterward—like I’ll get an artwork date wrong, or label something slightly incorrectly, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I swear I looked over the whole thing. And then I see it later and I’m just like, how did I miss that?

It’s not usually huge disasters, but it makes me feel kind of stupid, honestly. Like if I can spend forever fussing over the spacing or the wording, why am I still missing the basic thing? Then I get stuck thinking about it for way too long.

I don’t think so? I mean, not in a dramatic way. When I hear “impulse,” I think of someone suddenly quitting a job or buying a motorcycle or something, and that’s not really me.

I guess I can be impulsive with small things, like saying yes to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then again everyone does that sometimes. Or I’ll send a message and then reread it after and think, “Why did I phrase it like that?” Actually, emails are weird because I can spend ages on one and still send it at the wrong time or—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.

So… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed acting without thinking through consequences as a big pattern for me.

Yeah, I do get pretty upset with myself, especially when it’s something I feel like I should have caught.

Like, the thing that finally made me come in was I kept turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes—nothing dramatic, but embarrassing. I’d put the wrong date for an artwork, or label something slightly off, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I honestly thought I had checked everything.

And then I’ll notice it later and just feel awful about it. It’s frustrating because I’m like, I looked at this, how did I miss that? Then I kind of replay it in my head for a while.

So it feels like I’m busy with one general area, but I’m not always staying on the exact task I started with. Does that count, or do you mean like losing track of time on one specific thing?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted like this in other areas of your life, not just when you're working or studying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it happens outside of studying too, though the easiest example is still when I’m at my desk. Like I’ll be trying to read or write something and then a siren goes by outside, or I hear people talking in the hallway, and I’m just… off it.\n\nAnd sometimes it’s not even something real happening around me, it’s like a random thought. I’ll suddenly think about an exhibit idea or how I’d arrange something, and then I’m following that for, I don’t know, twenty minutes before I realize I completely stopped what I was doing. It’s kind of embarrassing because I thought I was still “working,” but I wasn’t really on the thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as especially restless, like I’m not pacing around all the time or anything.\n\nSitting still is… it depends. Actually, pens are kind of dangerous for me because I’ll start clicking one and then remember this one professor who banned them in class because everyone was clicking during exams—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nSo, um… maybe a little fidgety sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed or upset by your mistakes or distractions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially when it’s something I already thought I checked. That’s actually kind of what finally made me come in.\n\nI keep turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes that are really embarrassing afterward—like I’ll get an artwork date wrong, or label something slightly incorrectly, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I swear I looked over the whole thing. And then I see it later and I’m just like, how did I miss that?\n\nIt’s not usually huge disasters, but it makes me feel kind of stupid, honestly. Like if I can spend forever fussing over the spacing or the wording, why am I still missing the basic thing? Then I get stuck thinking about it for way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in a dramatic way. When I hear “impulse,” I think of someone suddenly quitting a job or buying a motorcycle or something, and that’s not really me.\n\nI guess I can be impulsive with small things, like saying yes to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then again everyone does that sometimes. I haven’t really noticed acting without thinking through consequences as a big pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel upset or distressed by your own thoughts or behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get pretty upset with myself, especially when it’s something I feel like I should have caught.\n\nLike, the thing that finally made me come in was I kept turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes—nothing dramatic, but embarrassing. Yeah, somewhat. I mean, I don’t think my friends are, like, mad at me or anything, but with work or school-type stuff it’s definitely started to show.

That’s actually kind of what finally made me come in.\n\nI keep turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes that are really embarrassing afterward—like I’ll get an artwork date wrong, or label something slightly incorrectly, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I swear I looked over the whole thing. And then I see it later and I’m just like, how did I miss that?\n\nIt’s not usually huge disasters, but it makes me feel kind of stupid, honestly. Like if I can spend forever fussing over the spacing or the wording, why am I still missing the basic thing? Then I get stuck thinking about it for way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in a dramatic way. Or I’ll send a message and then reread it after and think, “Why did I phrase it like that?” Actually, emails are weird because I can spend ages on one and still send it at the wrong time or—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed acting without thinking through consequences as a big pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel upset or distressed by your own thoughts or behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get pretty upset with myself, especially when it’s something I feel like I should have caught.\n\nLike, the thing that finally made me come in was I kept turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes—nothing dramatic, but embarrassing. I’d put the wrong date for an artwork, or label something slightly off, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I honestly thought I had checked everything.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice it later and just feel awful about it. Then I kind of replay it in my head for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have your mistakes, distractions, or difficulties with focus affected your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. Like if I can spend forever fussing over the spacing or the wording, why am I still missing the basic thing? Then I get stuck thinking about it for way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in a dramatic way. When I hear “impulse,” I think of someone suddenly quitting a job or buying a motorcycle or something, and that’s not really me.\n\nI guess I can be impulsive with small things, like saying yes to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then again everyone does that sometimes. Or I’ll send a message and then reread it after and think, “Why did I phrase it like that?” Actually, emails are weird because I can spend ages on one and still send it at the wrong time or—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed acting without thinking through consequences as a big pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel upset or distressed by your own thoughts or behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get pretty upset with myself, especially when it’s something I feel like I should have caught.\n\nLike, the thing that finally made me come in was I kept turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes—nothing dramatic, but embarrassing. I’d put the wrong date for an artwork, or label something slightly off, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I honestly thought I had checked everything.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice it later and just feel awful about it. It’s frustrating because I’m like, I looked at this, how did I miss that? Then I kind of replay it in my head for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have your mistakes, distractions, or difficulties with focus affected your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I mean, I don’t think my friends are, like, mad at me or anything, but with work or school-type stuff it’s definitely started to show.\n\nI’ve submitted things late because I got stuck or lost track of what still needed to be done, and I’ve missed a couple of application deadlines, which is… really frustrating because the applications are for things I actually care about. And one of my mentors basically told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for.\n\nThat kind of stuck with me. I keep thinking about it, because I don’t want people to see me as unreliable, but I can see why they might.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily life because of these mistakes or difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, daily-life-wise it’s shown up in kind of practical ways. That’s actually kind of what finally made me come in.\n\nI keep turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes that are really embarrassing afterward—like I’ll get an artwork date wrong, or label something slightly incorrectly, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I swear I looked over the whole thing. And then I see it later and I’m just like, how did I miss that?\n\nIt’s not usually huge disasters, but it makes me feel kind of stupid, honestly. Like if I can spend forever fussing over the spacing or the wording, why am I still missing the basic thing? Then I get stuck thinking about it for way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in a dramatic way. When I hear “impulse,” I think of someone suddenly quitting a job or buying a motorcycle or something, and that’s not really me.\n\nI guess I can be impulsive with small things, like saying yes to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then again everyone does that sometimes. Or I’ll send a message and then reread it after and think, “Why did I phrase it like that?” Actually, emails are weird because I can spend ages on one and still send it at the wrong time or—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed acting without thinking through consequences as a big pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel upset or distressed by your own thoughts or behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get pretty upset with myself, especially when it’s something I feel like I should have caught.\n\nLike, the thing that finally made me come in was I kept turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes—nothing dramatic, but embarrassing. I’d put the wrong date for an artwork, or label something slightly off, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I honestly thought I had checked everything.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice it later and just feel awful about it. It’s frustrating because I’m like, I looked at this, how did I miss that? Then I kind of replay it in my head for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have your mistakes, distractions, or difficulties with focus affected your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I mean, I don’t think my friends are, like, mad at me or anything, but with work or school-type stuff it’s definitely started to show.\n\nI’ve submitted things late because I got stuck or lost track of what still needed to be done, and I’ve missed a couple of application deadlines, which is… really frustrating because the applications are for things I actually care about. And one of my mentors basically told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for.\n\nThat kind of stuck with me. I keep thinking about it, because I don’t want people to see me as unreliable, but I can see why they might.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily life because of these mistakes or difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, daily-life-wise it’s shown up in kind of practical ways. I’ve turned things in late because I thought I was closer to done than I actually was, or I’d lose track of some piece of it.\n\nAnd I’ve missed a couple application deadlines, which still bothers me because those weren’t random things, they were opportunities I actually wanted. One mentor told me my follow-through was starting to get in the way of things I should be ready for, and that kind of hit hard.\n\nSo not like huge relationship blowups, but it does affect how people see me, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or down when you make these small mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Does that count, or do you mean like losing track of time on one specific thing?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted like this in other areas of your life, not just when you're working or studying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it happens outside of studying too, though the easiest example is still when I’m at my desk. Like I’ll be trying to read or write something and then a siren goes by outside, or I hear people talking in the hallway, and I’m just… off it.\n\nAnd sometimes it’s not even something real happening around me, it’s like a random thought. I’ll suddenly think about an exhibit idea or how I’d arrange something, and then I’m following that for, I don’t know, twenty minutes before I realize I completely stopped what I was doing. It’s kind of embarrassing because I thought I was still “working,” but I wasn’t really on the thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as especially restless, like I’m not pacing around all the time or anything.\n\nSitting still is… it depends. If I’m in a lecture or waiting somewhere, I might shift around or mess with a pen, but I don’t always notice it until someone points it out. Actually, pens are kind of dangerous for me because I’ll start clicking one and then remember this one professor who banned them in class because everyone was clicking during exams—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nSo, um… maybe a little fidgety sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed or upset by your mistakes or distractions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially when it’s something I already thought I checked. That’s actually kind of what finally made me come in.\n\nI keep turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes that are really embarrassing afterward—like I’ll get an artwork date wrong, or label something slightly incorrectly, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I swear I looked over the whole thing. And then I see it later and I’m just like, how did I miss that?\n\nIt’s not usually huge disasters, but it makes me feel kind of stupid, honestly. Like if I can spend forever fussing over the spacing or the wording, why am I still missing the basic thing? Then I get stuck thinking about it for way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in a dramatic way. When I hear “impulse,” I think of someone suddenly quitting a job or buying a motorcycle or something, and that’s not really me.\n\nI guess I can be impulsive with small things, like saying yes to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then again everyone does that sometimes. Or I’ll send a message and then reread it after and think, “Why did I phrase it like that?” Actually, emails are weird because I can spend ages on one and still send it at the wrong time or—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed acting without thinking through consequences as a big pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel upset or distressed by your own thoughts or behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get pretty upset with myself, especially when it’s something I feel like I should have caught.\n\nLike, the thing that finally made me come in was I kept turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes—nothing dramatic, but embarrassing. I’d put the wrong date for an artwork, or label something slightly off, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I honestly thought I had checked everything.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice it later and just feel awful about it. It’s frustrating because I’m like, I looked at this, how did I miss that? Then I kind of replay it in my head for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have your mistakes, distractions, or difficulties with focus affected your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I mean, I don’t think my friends are, like, mad at me or anything, but with work or school-type stuff it’s definitely started to show.\n\nI’ve submitted things late because I got stuck or lost track of what still needed to be done, and I’ve missed a couple of application deadlines, which is… really frustrating because the applications are for things I actually care about. And one of my mentors basically told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for.\n\nThat kind of stuck with me. I keep thinking about it, because I don’t want people to see me as unreliable, but I can see why they might.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily life because of these mistakes or difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, daily-life-wise it’s shown up in kind of practical ways. I’ve turned things in late because I thought I was closer to done than I actually was, or I’d lose track of some piece of it.\n\nAnd I’ve missed a couple application deadlines, which still bothers me because those weren’t random things, they were opportunities I actually wanted. One mentor told me my follow-through was starting to get in the way of things I should be ready for, and that kind of hit hard.\n\nSo not like huge relationship blowups, but it does affect how people see me, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or down when you make these small mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not like I’m sad for days or anything, but I get really down on myself in the moment.\n\nIt’s usually those small mistakes that get me—like I’ll turn in an application or research notes and later realize I put the wrong date for an artwork, or mislabeled something, or skipped a question even though I swear I checked it. And then I’m just like, how did I not see that?\n\nIt’s embarrassing, and I kind of spiral a little, because it feels so avoidable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take time off work or school because you were too overwhelmed or distracted to function?\n\nPatient: Not really like officially taking time off, I guess. I haven’t had to, like, stop going for a week or anything.\n\nBut it has made me fall behind in ways that kind of have the same effect. Like I’ve submitted work late because I got overwhelmed or distracted and didn’t keep track of what still needed finishing. And I missed a couple of application deadlines, which was really upsetting because they were things I actually wanted.\n\nOne of my mentors said my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for, and… yeah, that made it feel more serious than just “I’m a little scattered.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, F90.8, F41.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—so, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is I keep making these small mistakes in things I turn in, and they’re kind of embarrassing. Like applications or research notes, I’ll think I checked everything, and then later I notice I mislabeled an artwork date or skipped a question somehow.\n\nIt’s not like huge disasters, but it keeps happening enough that I’m starting to feel like, okay, why am I missing this stuff when I really did try to review it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you were paying attention when you were reviewing the information, or did your mind wander?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think I’m trying to pay attention, but yeah, my mind definitely gets pulled away. Like I’ll be at my desk reading or writing something, and a siren outside goes by, or someone’s talking in the hallway, and suddenly I’m kind of gone from what I was doing.\n\nOr even not something outside—sometimes I’ll have a random thought about an exhibit idea or how something should be arranged, and then I’m thinking about that for, like, twenty minutes before I realize I stopped reviewing the thing I was supposed to be checking. So I guess I’m looking at it, but I’m not always fully… there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Um… anxious? I don’t know. I mean, I get worried about normal stuff, I guess—like deadlines or whether I’ve messed something up. But I’m not sure I’d say it’s like… all the time.\n\nActually, it’s hard for me to tell sometimes because I’ll start thinking about one thing and then it turns into this whole other chain, like yesterday I was trying to remember if I replied to an email, and then I ended up looking at museum internship postings for an hour, which isn’t really anxiety, I don’t think. So… maybe some worry, but I haven’t really noticed it as a main thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself deeply focused on a single task or activity for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. Like, sometimes I’ll get really into something if it’s interesting, but I don’t know if I’d call it “deeply focused” in the way you mean.\n\nLike with art stuff, I can spend a long time looking up one artist or comparing exhibition layouts, and then suddenly I’ve got ten tabs open about something only kind of related. So it feels like I’m busy with one general area, but I’m not always staying on the exact task I started with. Does that count, or do you mean like losing track of time on one specific thing?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted like this in other areas of your life, not just when you're working or studying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it happens outside of studying too, though the easiest example is still when I’m at my desk. Like I’ll be trying to read or write something and then a siren goes by outside, or I hear people talking in the hallway, and I’m just… off it.\n\nAnd sometimes it’s not even something real happening around me, it’s like a random thought. I’ll suddenly think about an exhibit idea or how I’d arrange something, and then I’m following that for, I don’t know, twenty minutes before I realize I completely stopped what I was doing. It’s kind of embarrassing because I thought I was still “working,” but I wasn’t really on the thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as especially restless, like I’m not pacing around all the time or anything.\n\nSitting still is… it depends. If I’m in a lecture or waiting somewhere, I might shift around or mess with a pen, but I don’t always notice it until someone points it out. Actually, pens are kind of dangerous for me because I’ll start clicking one and then remember this one professor who banned them in class because everyone was clicking during exams—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nSo, um… maybe a little fidgety sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed or upset by your mistakes or distractions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially when it’s something I already thought I checked. That’s actually kind of what finally made me come in.\n\nI keep turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes that are really embarrassing afterward—like I’ll get an artwork date wrong, or label something slightly incorrectly, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I swear I looked over the whole thing. And then I see it later and I’m just like, how did I miss that?\n\nIt’s not usually huge disasters, but it makes me feel kind of stupid, honestly. Like if I can spend forever fussing over the spacing or the wording, why am I still missing the basic thing? Then I get stuck thinking about it for way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in a dramatic way. When I hear “impulse,” I think of someone suddenly quitting a job or buying a motorcycle or something, and that’s not really me.\n\nI guess I can be impulsive with small things, like saying yes to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then again everyone does that sometimes. Or I’ll send a message and then reread it after and think, “Why did I phrase it like that?” Actually, emails are weird because I can spend ages on one and still send it at the wrong time or—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed acting without thinking through consequences as a big pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel upset or distressed by your own thoughts or behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get pretty upset with myself, especially when it’s something I feel like I should have caught.\n\nLike, the thing that finally made me come in was I kept turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes—nothing dramatic, but embarrassing. I’d put the wrong date for an artwork, or label something slightly off, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I honestly thought I had checked everything.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice it later and just feel awful about it. It’s frustrating because I’m like, I looked at this, how did I miss that? Then I kind of replay it in my head for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have your mistakes, distractions, or difficulties with focus affected your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I mean, I don’t think my friends are, like, mad at me or anything, but with work or school-type stuff it’s definitely started to show.\n\nI’ve submitted things late because I got stuck or lost track of what still needed to be done, and I’ve missed a couple of application deadlines, which is… really frustrating because the applications are for things I actually care about. And one of my mentors basically told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for.\n\nThat kind of stuck with me. I keep thinking about it, because I don’t want people to see me as unreliable, but I can see why they might.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily life because of these mistakes or difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, daily-life-wise it’s shown up in kind of practical ways. I’ve turned things in late because I thought I was closer to done than I actually was, or I’d lose track of some piece of it.\n\nAnd I’ve missed a couple application deadlines, which still bothers me because those weren’t random things, they were opportunities I actually wanted. One mentor told me my follow-through was starting to get in the way of things I should be ready for, and that kind of hit hard.\n\nSo not like huge relationship blowups, but it does affect how people see me, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or down when you make these small mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not like I’m sad for days or anything, but I get really down on myself in the moment.\n\nIt’s usually those small mistakes that get me—like I’ll turn in an application or research notes and later realize I put the wrong date for an artwork, or mislabeled something, or skipped a question even though I swear I checked it. And then I’m just like, how did I not see that?\n\nIt’s embarrassing, and I kind of spiral a little, because it feels so avoidable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take time off work or school because you were too overwhelmed or distracted to function?\n\nPatient: Not really like officially taking time off, I guess. I haven’t had to, like, stop going for a week or anything.\n\nBut it has made me fall behind in ways that kind of have the same effect. Like I’ve submitted work late because I got overwhelmed or distracted and didn’t keep track of what still needed finishing. And I missed a couple of application deadlines, which was really upsetting because they were things I actually wanted.\n\nOne of my mentors said my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for, and… yeah, that made it feel more serious than just “I’m a little scattered.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your difficulties with focus and making mistakes are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, in a quieter way. Like it’s not that my whole life is falling apart, but it changes what I can actually take advantage of.\n\nBecause of it I’ve turned work in late, and I’ve missed a couple application deadlines — which still makes me feel stupid because I had the tabs open, I knew about them, I just didn’t… get it all the way done. And then one of my mentors basically told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess that does affect my quality of life. It makes me feel like I’m capable of more than what I’m actually managing to show.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F43.8", "F90.8", "F41.8"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Like it’s not that my whole life is falling apart, but it changes what I can actually take advantage of.\n\nBecause of it I’ve turned work in late, and I’ve missed a couple application deadlines — which still makes me feel stupid because I had the tabs open, I knew about them, I just didn’t… get it all the way done. And then one of my mentors basically told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess that does affect my quality of life. It makes me feel like I’m capable of more than what I’m actually managing to show.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been experiencing these difficulties with focus and making mistakes for a long time, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 21 / no_occupation / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been noticeable since I moved to Boston after finishing my bachelor’s, so it’s been going on for about 1 year and 11 months.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s not brand new, no. I mean, it became really noticeable after I moved to Boston, after I finished my bachelor’s. So… about almost two years now? Like, one year and eleven months, roughly.

Before that I probably had moments, but Boston was when I really started noticing it as a pattern, because everything was on me to keep track of.

Like I’ll be at my desk reading or writing something, and a siren outside goes by, or someone’s talking in the hallway, and suddenly I’m kind of gone from what I was doing.\n\nOr even not something outside—sometimes I’ll have a random thought about an exhibit idea or how something should be arranged, and then I’m thinking about that for, like, twenty minutes before I realize I stopped reviewing the thing I was supposed to be checking. So I guess I’m looking at it, but I’m not always fully… there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Um… anxious? I don’t know. I mean, I get worried about normal stuff, I guess—like deadlines or whether I’ve messed something up. But I’m not sure I’d say it’s like… all the time.\n\nActually, it’s hard for me to tell sometimes because I’ll start thinking about one thing and then it turns into this whole other chain, like yesterday I was trying to remember if I replied to an email, and then I ended up looking at museum internship postings for an hour, which isn’t really anxiety, I don’t think. So… maybe some worry, but I haven’t really noticed it as a main thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself deeply focused on a single task or activity for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. Like, sometimes I’ll get really into something if it’s interesting, but I don’t know if I’d call it “deeply focused” in the way you mean.\n\nLike with art stuff, I can spend a long time looking up one artist or comparing exhibition layouts, and then suddenly I’ve got ten tabs open about something only kind of related. So it feels like I’m busy with one general area, but I’m not always staying on the exact task I started with. Does that count, or do you mean like losing track of time on one specific thing?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted like this in other areas of your life, not just when you're working or studying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it happens outside of studying too, though the easiest example is still when I’m at my desk. Like I’ll be trying to read or write something and then a siren goes by outside, or I hear people talking in the hallway, and I’m just… off it.\n\nAnd sometimes it’s not even something real happening around me, it’s like a random thought. I’ll suddenly think about an exhibit idea or how I’d arrange something, and then I’m following that for, I don’t know, twenty minutes before I realize I completely stopped what I was doing. It’s kind of embarrassing because I thought I was still “working,” but I wasn’t really on the thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as especially restless, like I’m not pacing around all the time or anything.\n\nSitting still is… it depends. If I’m in a lecture or waiting somewhere, I might shift around or mess with a pen, but I don’t always notice it until someone points it out. Actually, pens are kind of dangerous for me because I’ll start clicking one and then remember this one professor who banned them in class because everyone was clicking during exams—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nSo, um… maybe a little fidgety sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed or upset by your mistakes or distractions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially when it’s something I already thought I checked. That’s actually kind of what finally made me come in.\n\nI keep turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes that are really embarrassing afterward—like I’ll get an artwork date wrong, or label something slightly incorrectly, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I swear I looked over the whole thing. And then I see it later and I’m just like, how did I miss that?\n\nIt’s not usually huge disasters, but it makes me feel kind of stupid, honestly. Like if I can spend forever fussing over the spacing or the wording, why am I still missing the basic thing? Then I get stuck thinking about it for way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in a dramatic way. When I hear “impulse,” I think of someone suddenly quitting a job or buying a motorcycle or something, and that’s not really me.\n\nI guess I can be impulsive with small things, like saying yes to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then again everyone does that sometimes. Or I’ll send a message and then reread it after and think, “Why did I phrase it like that?” Actually, emails are weird because I can spend ages on one and still send it at the wrong time or—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed acting without thinking through consequences as a big pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel upset or distressed by your own thoughts or behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get pretty upset with myself, especially when it’s something I feel like I should have caught.\n\nLike, the thing that finally made me come in was I kept turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes—nothing dramatic, but embarrassing. I’d put the wrong date for an artwork, or label something slightly off, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I honestly thought I had checked everything.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice it later and just feel awful about it. It’s frustrating because I’m like, I looked at this, how did I miss that? Then I kind of replay it in my head for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have your mistakes, distractions, or difficulties with focus affected your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I mean, I don’t think my friends are, like, mad at me or anything, but with work or school-type stuff it’s definitely started to show.\n\nI’ve submitted things late because I got stuck or lost track of what still needed to be done, and I’ve missed a couple of application deadlines, which is… really frustrating because the applications are for things I actually care about. And one of my mentors basically told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for.\n\nThat kind of stuck with me. I keep thinking about it, because I don’t want people to see me as unreliable, but I can see why they might.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily life because of these mistakes or difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, daily-life-wise it’s shown up in kind of practical ways. Not like I’m sad for days or anything, but I get really down on myself in the moment.\n\nIt’s usually those small mistakes that get me—like I’ll turn in an application or research notes and later realize I put the wrong date for an artwork, or mislabeled something, or skipped a question even though I swear I checked it. And then I’m just like, how did I not see that?\n\nIt’s embarrassing, and I kind of spiral a little, because it feels so avoidable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take time off work or school because you were too overwhelmed or distracted to function?\n\nPatient: Not really like officially taking time off, I guess. Like I’ve submitted work late because I got overwhelmed or distracted and didn’t keep track of what still needed finishing. for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I’ll mean to do normal adult things like return an email, pay a bill, or pick up groceries, and then days later I realize I never did it because it just dropped out of my mind.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: In seminars or gallery conversations I sometimes get excited and explain an idea for too long, then realize afterward that I barely left space for anyone else to respond.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, my teachers would write that I had good ideas but forgot to turn in worksheets, lost instructions, or missed little details unless my parents checked my backpack every night.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: Because of this, I’ve submitted work late, missed a couple of application deadlines, and had a mentor tell me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 11 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeable since I moved to Boston after finishing my bachelor’s, so it’s been going on for about 1 year and 11 months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 21 / no_occupation / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S001\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take time off work or school because you were too overwhelmed or distracted to function?\n\nPatient: Not really like officially taking time off, I guess. Like it’s not that my whole life is falling apart, but it changes what I can actually take advantage of.\n\nBecause of it I’ve turned work in late, and I’ve missed a couple application deadlines — which still makes me feel stupid because I had the tabs open, I knew about them, I just didn’t… get it all the way done. It makes me feel like I’m capable of more than what I’m actually managing to show.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these difficulties with focus and making mistakes for a long time, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: It’s not brand new, no. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more that I just lose focus. I mean, sometimes I’m worried about stuff, but a lot of the time it’s really random.

Like if I’m at my desk trying to read or write, a siren outside can pull me out of it, or someone talking in the hallway, and then suddenly I’m listening to that instead of what I was doing. Or I’ll have some random thought about an exhibit idea, and it feels relevant for a second, so I follow it… and then twenty minutes later I realize I haven’t touched the thing in front of me.

It’s not always like an anxiety spiral. It’s more like my attention just gets grabbed.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—so, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is I keep making these small mistakes in things I turn in, and they’re kind of embarrassing. Like applications or research notes, I’ll think I checked everything, and then later I notice I mislabeled an artwork date or skipped a question somehow.\n\nIt’s not like huge disasters, but it keeps happening enough that I’m starting to feel like, okay, why am I missing this stuff when I really did try to review it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you were paying attention when you were reviewing the information, or did your mind wander?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think I’m trying to pay attention, but yeah, my mind definitely gets pulled away. Like I’ll be at my desk reading or writing something, and a siren outside goes by, or someone’s talking in the hallway, and suddenly I’m kind of gone from what I was doing.\n\nOr even not something outside—sometimes I’ll have a random thought about an exhibit idea or how something should be arranged, and then I’m thinking about that for, like, twenty minutes before I realize I stopped reviewing the thing I was supposed to be checking. So I guess I’m looking at it, but I’m not always fully… there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Um… anxious? I don’t know. I mean, I get worried about normal stuff, I guess—like deadlines or whether I’ve messed something up. But I’m not sure I’d say it’s like… all the time.\n\nActually, it’s hard for me to tell sometimes because I’ll start thinking about one thing and then it turns into this whole other chain, like yesterday I was trying to remember if I replied to an email, and then I ended up looking at museum internship postings for an hour, which isn’t really anxiety, I don’t think. So… maybe some worry, but I haven’t really noticed it as a main thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself deeply focused on a single task or activity for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. Like, sometimes I’ll get really into something if it’s interesting, but I don’t know if I’d call it “deeply focused” in the way you mean.\n\nLike with art stuff, I can spend a long time looking up one artist or comparing exhibition layouts, and then suddenly I’ve got ten tabs open about something only kind of related. So it feels like I’m busy with one general area, but I’m not always staying on the exact task I started with. Does that count, or do you mean like losing track of time on one specific thing?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted like this in other areas of your life, not just when you're working or studying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it happens outside of studying too, though the easiest example is still when I’m at my desk. Like I’ll be trying to read or write something and then a siren goes by outside, or I hear people talking in the hallway, and I’m just… off it.\n\nAnd sometimes it’s not even something real happening around me, it’s like a random thought. I’ll suddenly think about an exhibit idea or how I’d arrange something, and then I’m following that for, I don’t know, twenty minutes before I realize I completely stopped what I was doing. It’s kind of embarrassing because I thought I was still “working,” but I wasn’t really on the thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as especially restless, like I’m not pacing around all the time or anything.\n\nSitting still is… it depends. If I’m in a lecture or waiting somewhere, I might shift around or mess with a pen, but I don’t always notice it until someone points it out. Actually, pens are kind of dangerous for me because I’ll start clicking one and then remember this one professor who banned them in class because everyone was clicking during exams—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nSo, um… maybe a little fidgety sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed or upset by your mistakes or distractions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially when it’s something I already thought I checked. That’s actually kind of what finally made me come in.\n\nI keep turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes that are really embarrassing afterward—like I’ll get an artwork date wrong, or label something slightly incorrectly, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I swear I looked over the whole thing. And then I see it later and I’m just like, how did I miss that?\n\nIt’s not usually huge disasters, but it makes me feel kind of stupid, honestly. Like if I can spend forever fussing over the spacing or the wording, why am I still missing the basic thing? Then I get stuck thinking about it for way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in a dramatic way. When I hear “impulse,” I think of someone suddenly quitting a job or buying a motorcycle or something, and that’s not really me.\n\nI guess I can be impulsive with small things, like saying yes to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then again everyone does that sometimes. Or I’ll send a message and then reread it after and think, “Why did I phrase it like that?” Actually, emails are weird because I can spend ages on one and still send it at the wrong time or—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed acting without thinking through consequences as a big pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel upset or distressed by your own thoughts or behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get pretty upset with myself, especially when it’s something I feel like I should have caught.\n\nLike, the thing that finally made me come in was I kept turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes—nothing dramatic, but embarrassing. I’d put the wrong date for an artwork, or label something slightly off, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I honestly thought I had checked everything.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice it later and just feel awful about it. It’s frustrating because I’m like, I looked at this, how did I miss that? Then I kind of replay it in my head for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have your mistakes, distractions, or difficulties with focus affected your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I mean, I don’t think my friends are, like, mad at me or anything, but with work or school-type stuff it’s definitely started to show.\n\nI’ve submitted things late because I got stuck or lost track of what still needed to be done, and I’ve missed a couple of application deadlines, which is… really frustrating because the applications are for things I actually care about. And one of my mentors basically told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for.\n\nThat kind of stuck with me. I keep thinking about it, because I don’t want people to see me as unreliable, but I can see why they might.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily life because of these mistakes or difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, daily-life-wise it’s shown up in kind of practical ways. I’ve turned things in late because I thought I was closer to done than I actually was, or I’d lose track of some piece of it.\n\nAnd I’ve missed a couple application deadlines, which still bothers me because those weren’t random things, they were opportunities I actually wanted. One mentor told me my follow-through was starting to get in the way of things I should be ready for, and that kind of hit hard.\n\nSo not like huge relationship blowups, but it does affect how people see me, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or down when you make these small mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not like I’m sad for days or anything, but I get really down on myself in the moment.\n\nIt’s usually those small mistakes that get me—like I’ll turn in an application or research notes and later realize I put the wrong date for an artwork, or mislabeled something, or skipped a question even though I swear I checked it. And then I’m just like, how did I not see that?\n\nIt’s embarrassing, and I kind of spiral a little, because it feels so avoidable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take time off work or school because you were too overwhelmed or distracted to function?\n\nPatient: Not really like officially taking time off, I guess. Like I’ve submitted work late because I got overwhelmed or distracted and didn’t keep track of what still needed finishing. It makes me feel like I’m capable of more than what I’m actually managing to show.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these difficulties with focus and making mistakes for a long time, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: It’s not brand new, no. One mentor told me my follow-through was starting to get in the way of things I should be ready for, and that kind of hit hard.\n\nSo not like huge relationship blowups, but it does affect how people see me, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or down when you make these small mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. And then one of my mentors basically told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess that does affect my quality of life. It makes me feel like I’m capable of more than what I’m actually managing to show.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these difficulties with focus and making mistakes for a long time, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: It’s not brand new, no. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—so, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is I keep making these small mistakes in things I turn in, and they’re kind of embarrassing. Like applications or research notes, I’ll think I checked everything, and then later I notice I mislabeled an artwork date or skipped a question somehow.\n\nIt’s not like huge disasters, but it keeps happening enough that I’m starting to feel like, okay, why am I missing this stuff when I really did try to review it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you were paying attention when you were reviewing the information, or did your mind wander?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think I’m trying to pay attention, but yeah, my mind definitely gets pulled away. Like I’ll be at my desk reading or writing something, and a siren outside goes by, or someone’s talking in the hallway, and suddenly I’m kind of gone from what I was doing.\n\nOr even not something outside—sometimes I’ll have a random thought about an exhibit idea or how something should be arranged, and then I’m thinking about that for, like, twenty minutes before I realize I stopped reviewing the thing I was supposed to be checking. So I guess I’m looking at it, but I’m not always fully… there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Um… anxious? I don’t know. I mean, I get worried about normal stuff, I guess—like deadlines or whether I’ve messed something up. But I’m not sure I’d say it’s like… all the time.\n\nActually, it’s hard for me to tell sometimes because I’ll start thinking about one thing and then it turns into this whole other chain, like yesterday I was trying to remember if I replied to an email, and then I ended up looking at museum internship postings for an hour, which isn’t really anxiety, I don’t think. So… maybe some worry, but I haven’t really noticed it as a main thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself deeply focused on a single task or activity for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. Like, sometimes I’ll get really into something if it’s interesting, but I don’t know if I’d call it “deeply focused” in the way you mean.\n\nLike with art stuff, I can spend a long time looking up one artist or comparing exhibition layouts, and then suddenly I’ve got ten tabs open about something only kind of related. So it feels like I’m busy with one general area, but I’m not always staying on the exact task I started with. Does that count, or do you mean like losing track of time on one specific thing?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted like this in other areas of your life, not just when you're working or studying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it happens outside of studying too, though the easiest example is still when I’m at my desk. Like I’ll be trying to read or write something and then a siren goes by outside, or I hear people talking in the hallway, and I’m just… off it.\n\nAnd sometimes it’s not even something real happening around me, it’s like a random thought. I’ll suddenly think about an exhibit idea or how I’d arrange something, and then I’m following that for, I don’t know, twenty minutes before I realize I completely stopped what I was doing. It’s kind of embarrassing because I thought I was still “working,” but I wasn’t really on the thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as especially restless, like I’m not pacing around all the time or anything.\n\nSitting still is… it depends. If I’m in a lecture or waiting somewhere, I might shift around or mess with a pen, but I don’t always notice it until someone points it out. Actually, pens are kind of dangerous for me because I’ll start clicking one and then remember this one professor who banned them in class because everyone was clicking during exams—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nSo, um… maybe a little fidgety sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed or upset by your mistakes or distractions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially when it’s something I already thought I checked. That’s actually kind of what finally made me come in.\n\nI keep turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes that are really embarrassing afterward—like I’ll get an artwork date wrong, or label something slightly incorrectly, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I swear I looked over the whole thing. And then I see it later and I’m just like, how did I miss that?\n\nIt’s not usually huge disasters, but it makes me feel kind of stupid, honestly. Like if I can spend forever fussing over the spacing or the wording, why am I still missing the basic thing? Then I get stuck thinking about it for way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in a dramatic way. When I hear “impulse,” I think of someone suddenly quitting a job or buying a motorcycle or something, and that’s not really me.\n\nI guess I can be impulsive with small things, like saying yes to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then again everyone does that sometimes. Or I’ll send a message and then reread it after and think, “Why did I phrase it like that?” Actually, emails are weird because I can spend ages on one and still send it at the wrong time or—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed acting without thinking through consequences as a big pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel upset or distressed by your own thoughts or behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get pretty upset with myself, especially when it’s something I feel like I should have caught.\n\nLike, the thing that finally made me come in was I kept turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes—nothing dramatic, but embarrassing. I’d put the wrong date for an artwork, or label something slightly off, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I honestly thought I had checked everything.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice it later and just feel awful about it. It’s frustrating because I’m like, I looked at this, how did I miss that? Then I kind of replay it in my head for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have your mistakes, distractions, or difficulties with focus affected your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I mean, I don’t think my friends are, like, mad at me or anything, but with work or school-type stuff it’s definitely started to show.\n\nI’ve submitted things late because I got stuck or lost track of what still needed to be done, and I’ve missed a couple of application deadlines, which is… really frustrating because the applications are for things I actually care about. And one of my mentors basically told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for.\n\nThat kind of stuck with me. I keep thinking about it, because I don’t want people to see me as unreliable, but I can see why they might.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily life because of these mistakes or difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, daily-life-wise it’s shown up in kind of practical ways. I’ve turned things in late because I thought I was closer to done than I actually was, or I’d lose track of some piece of it.\n\nAnd I’ve missed a couple application deadlines, which still bothers me because those weren’t random things, they were opportunities I actually wanted. One mentor told me my follow-through was starting to get in the way of things I should be ready for, and that kind of hit hard.\n\nSo not like huge relationship blowups, but it does affect how people see me, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or down when you make these small mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not like I’m sad for days or anything, but I get really down on myself in the moment.\n\nIt’s usually those small mistakes that get me—like I’ll turn in an application or research notes and later realize I put the wrong date for an artwork, or mislabeled something, or skipped a question even though I swear I checked it. And then I’m just like, how did I not see that?\n\nIt’s embarrassing, and I kind of spiral a little, because it feels so avoidable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take time off work or school because you were too overwhelmed or distracted to function?\n\nPatient: Not really like officially taking time off, I guess. I haven’t had to, like, stop going for a week or anything.\n\nBut it has made me fall behind in ways that kind of have the same effect. Like I’ve submitted work late because I got overwhelmed or distracted and didn’t keep track of what still needed finishing. And I missed a couple of application deadlines, which was really upsetting because they were things I actually wanted.\n\nOne of my mentors said my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for, and… yeah, that made it feel more serious than just “I’m a little scattered.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your difficulties with focus and making mistakes are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, in a quieter way. Like it’s not that my whole life is falling apart, but it changes what I can actually take advantage of.\n\nBecause of it I’ve turned work in late, and I’ve missed a couple application deadlines — which still makes me feel stupid because I had the tabs open, I knew about them, I just didn’t… get it all the way done. And then one of my mentors basically told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess that does affect my quality of life. It makes me feel like I’m capable of more than what I’m actually managing to show.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these difficulties with focus and making mistakes for a long time, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: It’s not brand new, no. I mean, it became really noticeable after I moved to Boston, after I finished my bachelor’s. So… about almost two years now? Like, one year and eleven months, roughly.\n\nBefore that I probably had moments, but Boston was when I really started noticing it as a pattern, because everything was on me to keep track of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wanders or gets distracted because you're worried about something, or because you just lose focus on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I just lose focus. I mean, sometimes I’m worried about stuff, but a lot of the time it’s really random.\n\nLike if I’m at my desk trying to read or write, a siren outside can pull me out of it, or someone talking in the hallway, and then suddenly I’m listening to that instead of what I was doing. Or I’ll have some random thought about an exhibit idea, and it feels relevant for a second, so I follow it… and then twenty minutes later I realize I haven’t touched the thing in front of me.\n\nIt’s not always like an anxiety spiral. It’s more like my attention just gets grabbed.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulties with focus and mistakes are significantly interfering with your ability to achieve your goals or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not in every part of my life, like I’m not losing all my relationships or anything, but goal-wise it definitely gets in the way.\n\nI’ve turned things in late, and I missed a couple application deadlines, which is frustrating because I was aware of them — I just didn’t get from “knowing about it” to actually submitting. And one of my mentors said my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for.\n\nThat kind of stuck with me, because I do want those things. I’m just… not consistently getting myself across the finish line.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Not usually, I don’t think. I mean, I can get worried about deadlines and stuff, but the focus thing happens even when I’m not feeling especially anxious.

When I hear “impulse,” I think of someone suddenly quitting a job or buying a motorcycle or something, and that’s not really me.\n\nI guess I can be impulsive with small things, like saying yes to something before I’ve really looked at my schedule, but then again everyone does that sometimes. Or I’ll send a message and then reread it after and think, “Why did I phrase it like that?” Actually, emails are weird because I can spend ages on one and still send it at the wrong time or—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed acting without thinking through consequences as a big pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel upset or distressed by your own thoughts or behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get pretty upset with myself, especially when it’s something I feel like I should have caught.\n\nLike, the thing that finally made me come in was I kept turning in applications or research notes with these small mistakes—nothing dramatic, but embarrassing. I’d put the wrong date for an artwork, or label something slightly off, or somehow skip a question on a form even though I honestly thought I had checked everything.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice it later and just feel awful about it. It’s frustrating because I’m like, I looked at this, how did I miss that? Then I kind of replay it in my head for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have your mistakes, distractions, or difficulties with focus affected your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I mean, I don’t think my friends are, like, mad at me or anything, but with work or school-type stuff it’s definitely started to show.\n\nI’ve submitted things late because I got stuck or lost track of what still needed to be done, and I’ve missed a couple of application deadlines, which is… really frustrating because the applications are for things I actually care about. And one of my mentors basically told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for.\n\nThat kind of stuck with me. I keep thinking about it, because I don’t want people to see me as unreliable, but I can see why they might.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily life because of these mistakes or difficulties with focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, daily-life-wise it’s shown up in kind of practical ways. I’ve turned things in late because I thought I was closer to done than I actually was, or I’d lose track of some piece of it.\n\nAnd I’ve missed a couple application deadlines, which still bothers me because those weren’t random things, they were opportunities I actually wanted. One mentor told me my follow-through was starting to get in the way of things I should be ready for, and that kind of hit hard.\n\nSo not like huge relationship blowups, but it does affect how people see me, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or down when you make these small mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not like I’m sad for days or anything, but I get really down on myself in the moment.\n\nIt’s usually those small mistakes that get me—like I’ll turn in an application or research notes and later realize I put the wrong date for an artwork, or mislabeled something, or skipped a question even though I swear I checked it. And then I’m just like, how did I not see that?\n\nIt’s embarrassing, and I kind of spiral a little, because it feels so avoidable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take time off work or school because you were too overwhelmed or distracted to function?\n\nPatient: Not really like officially taking time off, I guess. I haven’t had to, like, stop going for a week or anything.\n\nBut it has made me fall behind in ways that kind of have the same effect. Like I’ve submitted work late because I got overwhelmed or distracted and didn’t keep track of what still needed finishing. And I missed a couple of application deadlines, which was really upsetting because they were things I actually wanted.\n\nOne of my mentors said my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for, and… yeah, that made it feel more serious than just “I’m a little scattered.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your difficulties with focus and making mistakes are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, in a quieter way. Like it’s not that my whole life is falling apart, but it changes what I can actually take advantage of.\n\nBecause of it I’ve turned work in late, and I’ve missed a couple application deadlines — which still makes me feel stupid because I had the tabs open, I knew about them, I just didn’t… get it all the way done. And then one of my mentors basically told me my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess that does affect my quality of life. It makes me feel like I’m capable of more than what I’m actually managing to show.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these difficulties with focus and making mistakes for a long time, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: It’s not brand new, no. I mean, it became really noticeable after I moved to Boston, after I finished my bachelor’s. So… about almost two years now? Like, one year and eleven months, roughly.\n\nBefore that I probably had moments, but Boston was when I really started noticing it as a pattern, because everything was on me to keep track of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wanders or gets distracted because you're worried about something, or because you just lose focus on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I just lose focus. I mean, sometimes I’m worried about stuff, but a lot of the time it’s really random.\n\nLike if I’m at my desk trying to read or write, a siren outside can pull me out of it, or someone talking in the hallway, and then suddenly I’m listening to that instead of what I was doing. Or I’ll have some random thought about an exhibit idea, and it feels relevant for a second, so I follow it… and then twenty minutes later I realize I haven’t touched the thing in front of me.\n\nIt’s not always like an anxiety spiral. It’s more like my attention just gets grabbed.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulties with focus and mistakes are significantly interfering with your ability to achieve your goals or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not in every part of my life, like I’m not losing all my relationships or anything, but goal-wise it definitely gets in the way.\n\nI’ve turned things in late, and I missed a couple application deadlines, which is frustrating because I was aware of them — I just didn’t get from “knowing about it” to actually submitting. And one of my mentors said my follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities I should be ready for.\n\nThat kind of stuck with me, because I do want those things. I’m just… not consistently getting myself across the finish line.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and mistakes are usually triggered by feeling anxious or worried about something?\n\nPatient: Not usually, I don’t think. I mean, I can get worried about deadlines and stuff, but the focus thing happens even when I’m not feeling especially anxious.\n\nLike if I’m trying to read or write at my desk, it can be something really small — a siren outside, or people talking in the hallway — and then I’m just… gone from what I was doing. Or I’ll have some random thought about an exhibit idea, which is funny because sometimes those ideas are actually interesting, but then I follow that for a while, and suddenly twenty minutes have passed and I haven’t done the thing I sat down to do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
